## Desloppify: An Aesthetic Critique

After exploring this codebase thoroughly, I see a project with genuine architectural discipline and real thought behind layering, but one that suffers from several aesthetic and expressive problems that keep it from feeling truly beautiful. Here's my opinionated assessment:

### 1. **Import Ceremony and Facade Redundancy**

The codebase exhibits a pattern of unnecessary wrapping that creates dissonance. Look at `/base/text/text_api.py`:

```python
def get_project_root() -> Path:
    """Return the active project root as an absolute resolved path."""
    return text_utils_mod.get_project_root().resolve()
```

This is a wrapper that wraps a wrapper that wraps an implementation. The same pattern repeats throughout: `text/text_api.py` wraps `text_utils.py`, which is already in `base/`. Why not just import directly from `text_utils`? The facade pretends to solve a problem—decoupling from implementation details—but since `text_api` is itself a public API, it's just added distance.

Worse: you have `base/discovery/api.py` that's purely a re-export facade for `base/discovery/source.py` and `base/discovery/file_paths.py`. Each function is re-exported with zero transformation:

```python
from desloppify.base.discovery.source import (
    DEFAULT_EXCLUSIONS,
    clear_source_file_cache_for_tests,
    collect_exclude_dirs,
    # ... 10 more direct imports
)
```

This is *not* an abstraction; it's a **re-export ceremony**. The comment in `CLAUDE.md` says "No backward compatibility for import paths or APIs" yet these facade modules exist purely to maintain old import paths. They should either be removed or actually provide value through selective re-exporting (filtering, transformation, conceptual grouping).

### 2. **Naming: Precision Lost in Prefixes and Underscores**

The codebase is **inconsistent** about when it uses leading underscores for "private" vs when it uses explicit naming.

Look at `cli.py`:
```python
_DETECTOR_NAMES_CACHE
_get_detector_names()
_invalidate_detector_names_cache()
_apply_persisted_exclusions()
_resolve_default_path()
_load_shared_runtime()
_resolve_handler()
_handle_help_command()
```

Every single helper in the main CLI module is prefixed with `_`. This isn't unusual, but combined with the next layer down (registry.py, commands, etc.), it creates a **naming burden**. There's no consistent rule: some modules use underscore-prefix to mean "internal to this module," others use it to mean "detail function," others use it to mean "step in a process."

Compare this with `intelligence/narrative/_constants.py`:
```python
_DETECTOR_TOOLS = _detector_tools()
_refresh_detector_tools()
_DETECTOR_CASCADE
_REMINDER_DECAY_THRESHOLD
_FEEDBACK_URL
```

The `_` here means "this is a module-internal cache or config detail that shouldn't be imported." But in `cli.py`, it just means "this is a helper function." The inconsistency makes the code feel **edgy and uncertain**.

### 3. **Cascading Underscore Imports Create Illegible Code**

In `intelligence/narrative/core.py`, watch what happens:

```python
from desloppify.intelligence.narrative.signals import (
    compute_badge_status as _compute_badge_status,
)
from desloppify.intelligence.narrative.signals import (
    compute_primary_action as _compute_primary_action,
)
from desloppify.intelligence.narrative.signals import (
    compute_risk_flags as _compute_risk_flags,
)
# ... 6 more similar imports
```

Each import gets aliased to an underscore version for... what reason? To mark them as implementation details? But they're called directly in `compute_narrative()`:

```python
badge = _compute_badge_status()
phase = _detect_phase(history, strict_score)
dimensions = _analyze_dimensions(dim_scores, history, state)
```

This **looks cluttered**. The reader sees `_compute_badge_status()` and wonders: is this a private function? Why does it have an underscore? The answer is: "so we can import it from another module." That's not a good reason. If you're re-aliasing everything, either:
- Use the original names and drop the aliases
- Or explain in a docstring why the aliasing is necessary

The underscore adds visual noise without clarity.

### 4. **The `state.py` Re-Export Monster**

`/desloppify/state.py` is a **220-line re-export hub**:

```python
from desloppify.engine._state.filtering import (
    add_ignore,
    is_ignored,
    issue_in_scan_scope,
    # ...
)
from desloppify.engine._state.merge import (
    MergeScanOptions,
    find_suspect_detectors,
    merge_scan,
    # ...
)
# ... 9 more submodule imports
```

Then exports everything in `__all__`. This is a **façade** that makes sense architecturally—it provides a stable public API—but it's *exhausting to maintain*. Every time engine/_state changes, this file must change. And there's no filtering: every internal function is bubbled up to the public API. The file doesn't *choose* what to expose; it just vacuums up everything.

Compare this to `engine/plan.py`, which does something similar but at least **documents its organization**:

```python
# --- auto-clustering --------------------------------------------------------
from desloppify.engine._plan.auto_cluster import (
    AUTO_PREFIX,
    auto_cluster_issues,
)
# --- commit tracking --------------------------------------------------------
from desloppify.engine._plan.commit_tracking import (
    # ...
)
```

At least `plan.py` uses **section headers** to organize re-exports conceptually. `state.py` does not. It's just a wall of imports.

### 5. **Constants Live in Multiple Places (Semantic Drift)**

The codebase has configuration living in scattered, sometimes redundant locations:

- `base/enums.py` has `_CANONICAL_ISSUE_STATUSES`, `_RESOLVED_STATUSES`, `_LEGACY_STATUS_ALIASES`
- `base/registry.py` has `DISPLAY_ORDER`, `DETECTORS`, and `_ACTION_PRIORITY`, `_ACTION_LABELS`
- `intelligence/narrative/_constants.py` has `DETECTOR_TOOLS`, `STRUCTURAL_MERGE`, `_DETECTOR_CASCADE`, `_REMINDER_DECAY_THRESHOLD`
- `languages/python/phases.py` has `PY_COMPLEXITY_SIGNALS`, `PY_GOD_RULES`, `PY_SKIP_NAMES`, `PY_ENTRY_PATTERNS`
- `base/config.py` has `CONFIG_SCHEMA` with 10+ configuration keys

Each of these is **isolated and well-named**, but there's **no clear hierarchy**. When you need to understand the full universe of rules and signals, you need to grep across 5+ files. There's no "system constants" document, no single source of truth for configuration that's easy to survey.

### 6. **Module Names Don't Match Their Purpose (Discovery Layer Confusion)**

The `base/discovery/` package contains:
- `api.py` — re-export facade
- `source.py` — file finding + caching
- `file_paths.py` — path resolution + matching
- `paths.py` — project root resolution
- `scan.py` — unknown (didn't check)
- `path_io.py` — unknown (didn't check)

The naming is **redundant and unclear**. Why `file_paths.py` AND `paths.py`? Both deal with paths. The names don't tell you what's in each. A reader can't guess: does `source.py` have file-finding logic or source-code parsing? The answer is file-finding, but the name suggests otherwise.

Compare with a clearer structure (hypothetical):
- `source_files.py` — finding Python/TypeScript files
- `path_resolution.py` — resolving relative to project root
- `path_exclusions.py` — matching against exclusion patterns

Each name would be **specific and predictable**.

### 7. **The `_internal` Folder Exists but Isn't Clear**

`base/_internal/` exists (mentioned in git status as "promote _internal/"). But it's unclear what lives there and why. If it's a reorganization in progress, fine. But the presence of a `_internal` folder alongside public modules signals **incomplete architecture**. Either integrate it or document what it is.

### 8. **Command Handlers Don't Have a Consistent Interface Pattern**

Look at the command registry (`app/commands/registry.py`):

```python
def _build_handlers() -> dict[str, CommandHandler]:
    from desloppify.app.commands.config import cmd_config
    from desloppify.app.commands.detect import cmd_detect
    from desloppify.app.commands.dev import cmd_dev
    # ...
    return {
        "scan": cmd_scan,
        "status": cmd_status,
        "show": cmd_show,
        "next": cmd_next,
        # ...
    }
```

This works, but there's **no type signature** or documented contract for what a command handler is. Looking at `next/cmd.py`:

```python
def cmd_next(args: argparse.Namespace) -> None:
    """next command: show next highest-priority queue items."""
```

And `scan/cmd.py`:

```python
def cmd_scan(args: argparse.Namespace) -> None:
    """scan command: run all detectors, update persistent state, show diff."""
```

The **signature is the same**, the **docstring format is the same**, but there's no central `CommandHandler` protocol or base class to document this. The type alias exists:

```python
CommandHandler = Callable[[Any], None]
```

But it uses `Any` instead of `argparse.Namespace`. This is **imprecise**. A strict type would make the interface clearer:

```python
CommandHandler = Callable[[argparse.Namespace], None]
```

### 9. **Test Coverage: The Naming Problem**

You have files named:
- `test_coverage.py` in `languages/python/`
- `coverage/` folder in `engine/detectors/`
- `test_coverage` detector in the registry

This creates **collision in the semantic namespace**. Is "test_coverage" a noun (coverage of tests) or a verb (testing coverage)? The detector is about finding untested code, not about measuring test coverage. The naming is backwards. A clearer name might be "untested" or "low_coverage" as a detector, distinct from the `test_coverage_hooks` module.

### 10. **The Runtime-Context Pattern is Hidden**

Looking at `cli.py` and command execution:

```python
def _load_shared_runtime(args) -> None:
    """Load config/state and attach shared objects to parsed args."""
    config = load_config()
    state_file = state_path(args)
    state = load_state(state_file)
    _apply_persisted_exclusions(args, config)
    args.runtime = CommandRuntime(config=config, state=state, state_path=state_file)
```

This **mutates the args namespace** to attach a `runtime` attribute. It's a common pattern in argparse-based CLIs, but it's **implicit and surprising**. A command handler receives an `args` object and has to know (via undocumented convention) that there's a `.runtime` attribute available. 

Better would be an explicit pattern:

```python
def _load_shared_runtime(args) -> CommandRuntime:
    # Return the runtime; let the caller attach it
    ...

# In main():
runtime = _load_shared_runtime(args)
handler(args, runtime=runtime)
```

Or use a proper dependency-injection container. The current approach is **convenient but hidden**.

### 11. **Narrative Computation is a Black Box (No Clear Data Flow)**

In `intelligence/narrative/core.py`, the `compute_narrative()` function calls 15+ helper functions and returns a massive dict. Tracing the data flow requires reading code across 6 files:

```python
by_detector = _count_open_by_detector(issues)
badge = _compute_badge_status()
phase = _detect_phase(history, strict_score)
dimensions = _analyze_dimensions(dim_scores, history, state)
debt = _analyze_debt(dim_scores, issues, history)
# ... 10 more computations
```

Each function operates on overlapping state, and it's **not obvious** what each returns or how dependencies flow. There's no data structure capturing "all the things needed to compute narrative" or "all the outputs from narrative." The function is **procedurally clear** but **architecturally opaque**.

A TypedDict or dataclass for `NarrativeInputs` and `NarrativeOutputs` would help:

```python
@dataclass
class NarrativeInputs:
    state: StateModel
    diff: dict | None
    lang: str | None
    # ...

@dataclass  
class NarrativeOutputs:
    phase: str
    headline: str
    dimensions: list
    # ...

def compute_narrative(inputs: NarrativeInputs) -> NarrativeOutputs:
    ...
```

### 12. **Signal Functions Create Fragmentation**

The `intelligence/narrative/signals.py` file (mentioned but not read) appears to be doing **two separate jobs**: computing signals and extracting data. Functions like `_score_snapshot`, `_count_open_by_detector`, `_scoped_issues` sound like data extraction, while `compute_badge_status` sounds like signal computation. The naming suggests they belong in different files.

### 13. **Inconsistent Use of Dataclasses**

Some modules use TypedDicts (state.py, various command options), others use dataclasses (`FunctionInfo`, `ClassInfo`, `ComplexitySignal`, `GodRule`, `CommandRuntime`, `NarrativeContext`).

For read-only settings/configuration, TypedDict is fine. For things with methods or invariants, dataclass is better. But the codebase **mixes both liberally**. Look at `NextOptions`:

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class NextOptions:
    count: int = 1
    scope: str | None = None
    # ...
    
    @classmethod
    def from_args(cls, args: argparse.Namespace) -> NextOptions:
        return cls(
            count=getattr(args, "count", 1) or 1,
            scope=getattr(args, "scope", None),
            # ...
        )
```

This is good! It's a clear, validated container. But then other commands don't follow this pattern. Scan uses dictionaries. Show uses dictionaries. There's **no consistency** on when to use a dataclass vs a dict.

### 14. **Deep Nesting in `engine/_plan/` and `engine/_state/`**

The folder structure is:
```
engine/
  _plan/
    auto_cluster.py
    commit_tracking.py
    epic_triage.py
    operations.py
    persistence.py
    reconcile.py
    schema.py
    stale_dimensions.py
    subjective_policy.py
  _state/
    filtering.py
    merge.py
    noise.py
    persistence.py
    resolution.py
    schema.py
    schema_scores.py
    scoring.py
```

The `_` prefix signals "private," but then `engine/plan.py` and `desloppify/state.py` **re-export everything**. This doesn't feel private; it feels **partitioned for organization's sake**. If these are truly internal, they should be import-restricted. If they're meant to be used, they should be in `plan/` and `state/` (no underscore).

The middle ground—hide it with `_` but export it anyway—feels **indecisive**.

### 15. **The "Helpers" Namespace Pollution**

`app/commands/helpers/` contains 13 files. Some are tightly focused (`display.py`, `lang.py`), others are grab-bags (`queue_progress.py` is 400 lines, `rendering.py`). There's no clear taxonomy: is a "helper" something that's shared across commands, or something that's a utility function, or something that's a partial implementation of command logic?

The folder should either be split by *what it helps with* (scan_helpers/, show_helpers/) or renamed to reflect its actual purpose (command_utilities/, command_infrastructure/).

### 16. **Text Utilities Don't Match Their Names**

`base/text_utils.py` contains:
- `get_project_root()` — not about text
- `read_code_snippet()` — text, but really code reading
- `get_area()` — path manipulation, not text
- `strip_c_style_comments()` — actually text processing
- `is_numeric()` — type checking, not text

The module name is **misleading**. It should be `text_and_path_utils.py` or split into `text_utils.py` and `file_utils.py`. Most of the functions are about paths and code, not text.

### 17. **Scoring Feels Scattered (Multiple Score Types)**

The codebase has:
- `overall_score`
- `objective_score`
- `strict_score`
- `verified_strict_score`

These are computed in `engine/_state/schema_scores.py` via `_get_overall_score()`, `_get_objective_score()`, etc., and bundled into `ScoreSnapshot` in `state.py`. But the **purpose and relationship** between these four scores is never explicitly documented in the code. A reader must infer it from usage.

A central "scoring model" document or a dataclass with explanatory docstrings would clarify:

```python
@dataclass
class Scores:
    """Four canonical health scores.
    
    overall: Weighted average across all dimensions.
    objective: Based on issue counts only (no subjective assessment).
    strict: Highest standard (excludes issues below medium confidence).
    verified: Strict score, verified by recent review.
    """
    overall: float | None
    objective: float | None
    strict: float | None
    verified: float | None
```

### 18. **The "Intelligence" Package Boundary is Blurry**

The `intelligence/` package has three subpackages: `narrative`, `review`, `integrity`. But they're **disconnected**. Narrative computes coaching, review prepares feedback, integrity checks validity. What's the conceptual unity? The docstring in `__init__.py` lists them, but doesn't explain when to use each or how they relate.

Better would be a central entry point or coordinator that shows the full workflow:

```python
"""Intelligence layer: subjective code review and coaching.

Workflow:
1. prepare_review() — gather review context
2. import_review_issues() — integrate LLM feedback
3. check_integrity() — validate consistency
4. compute_narrative() — derive coaching
"""
```

### 19. **Language Plugins Are Opaque (Plugin Architecture)**

The language system registers plugins dynamically:

```python
@register_lang(name)
def decorator(cls: T) -> T:
    validate_lang_structure(Path(module.__file__).parent, name)
    # ...
    registry_state.register(name, cfg)
```

This works, but **you can't easily survey what languages are available** without running the code. Each language folder has a different structure (some with tests, some without). There's a `_framework` folder with shared code, but it's **not obvious what's required** vs optional for a language plugin.

Documentation would help: "To add a language: create `languages/mylang/` with files `__init__.py`, `detectors/`, `phases.py`" etc.

### 20. **The "Zones" Concept Isn't Named Consistently**

The codebase has:
- `engine/policy/zones.py` — zone classification
- `engine/policy/zones.py` has a `Zone` enum
- Zone rules are sprinkled in language configs (`PY_ZONE_RULES`, `TS_ZONE_RULES`)

The concept is clear, but the **naming is scattered**. You have `Zone.PRODUCTION`, `Zone.TEST`, `Zone.CONFIG`, etc., but they're defined in one file and used in multiple places. A clearer pattern would be:

```python
# zones.py
class FileZone(enum.Enum):
    """Classification of a file's purpose in the codebase."""
    PRODUCTION = "production"
    # ...

# In each language:
ZONE_CLASSIFICATION_RULES = [
    ZoneRule(FileZone.PRODUCTION, ["src/"]),
    # ...
]
```

Explicit naming (`FileZone` vs `Zone`) makes it clearer what's being classified.

### Summary

The codebase is **well-engineered architecturally** but **aesthetically dissonant**. The problems aren't bugs; they're friction:

1. **Too many re-export facades** that add distance without value
2. **Inconsistent underscore conventions** for "private"
3. **Naming precision lost** (discovery/, text_utils, signals)
4. **Data structures not matching their purpose** (state.py as a re-export hub)
5. **Hidden patterns** (runtime as mutated args attribute)
6. **Scattered constants** across many files
7. **Inconsistent typing** (TypedDict vs dataclass)
8. **Architectural indecision** (is `_plan` private or not?)

The fix isn't big refactoring; it's **thoughtful simplification**:
- Kill the re-export facades or make them conceptually valuable
- Standardize on when underscore means "private" vs "internal step"
- Rename modules to match their actual content
- Consolidate related constants
- Add central documentation explaining the data flow (narratives, scoring, zones)
- Use dataclasses for all configuration/bundled data
- Be decisive about what's truly private vs public

The code *works beautifully*; it just doesn't *feel* beautiful. That's fixable without rewriting.

## Systematic Catalog of Design Issues in Desloppify

Based on thorough exploration of the codebase, here are the structural and design-level issues that reduce rigor and precision:

---

### 1. **IMPLICIT STATE MUTATIONS WITH UNCLEAR CONTRACTS**

**Problem**: Core functions that mutate state/plan dictionaries in-place but return `None` or don't explicitly signal what changed. This violates the principle that functions should either be pure (return a new value) or clearly side-effectful (obvious by name/doc).

**Specific Examples**:

- **`/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_state/schema.py:244`** (`ensure_state_defaults`)  
  - Signature: `def ensure_state_defaults(state: StateModel | dict) -> None:`
  - Mutates `state` in-place (171 lines of mutation), returns `None`. Caller can't distinguish between "success with no changes" vs "changed X fields" vs "discarded Y issues".
  - Line 305: `return None` is explicit but semantically empty.

- **`/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_state/schema.py:223`** (`migrate_state_keys`)  
  - Mutates multiple nested dicts in-place, returns `bool` on *first* rename only (line 220), but not on subsequent ones. Inconsistent.

- **`/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_state/merge_history.py:9-45`** (`_record_scan_metadata`, `_merge_scan_inputs`)  
  - Both mutate `state` in-place with no return. Line 22-23 calls `.setdefault()` then directly indexes into the result.

- **`/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_plan/reconcile.py:58-114`** (`_supersede_id`)  
  - Mutates plan in 8 different ways (lines 94, 99-100, 101, 102-106, 110-112). Returns `bool` but that's only meaningful if you read the entire function.

**Impact**: Callers can't reason about side effects without reading full function bodies. Testing requires state inspection, not assertion of return values.

---

### 2. **IMPLICIT OPTIONAL PARAMETERS WITH RUNTIME DEFENSIVENESS**

**Problem**: Parameters are sometimes optional in signature but treated as required in code; sometimes passed as required but tested with `.get()` as if optional. No Type-based contract enforcement.

**Specific Examples**:

- **`/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_state/merge_issues.py:65-75`** (`auto_resolve_disappeared`)
  - Parameters `scan_path: str | None` and `exclude: tuple[str, ...] = ()` are both optional.
  - Lines 97-100: Treats `scan_path` defensively even though it's a typed parameter. If you pass None, line 98 doesn't raise—it silently skips the check.
  - Caller responsibility to understand: what does `scan_path=None` mean? Match everything? Is that the intent?

- **`/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_state/filtering.py:29-50`** (`path_scoped_issues`, `issue_in_scan_scope`)
  - `scan_path: str | None` is optional, but logic (line 43-50) relies on implicit defaults: "if not scan_path or scan_path == '.', return True".
  - Caller doesn't know: does None mean "all files"? Does "." mean "all files"? Are they equivalent?

- **`/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_plan/schema.py:244`** (ItemOverride TypedDict)
  - `cluster: str | None` is optional. But is None meaningful? Does it mean "no cluster" or "cluster unknown"? Nowhere specified.

**Impact**: Subtle bugs when None-handling assumptions diverge. No static guarantee that optional params are actually handled safely.

---

### 3. **MULTIPLE DATA REPRESENTATIONS FOR SAME CONCEPT**

**Problem**: The same concept (a metric, a score, a dimension) is represented differently in different modules, forcing repeated conversions and opening up inconsistencies.

**Specific Examples**:

- **Subjective Dimension Scores**:
  - In state: `state["subjective_assessments"][dimension_key]` is either a dict with `{"score": float}` or a plain `float` (line 58-62, `_coerce_subjective_score` in `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_state/scoring.py`).
  - Also appears as `SubjectiveAssessment` TypedDict in `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_state/schema.py:122-131` with fields `score`, `integrity_penalty`, `provisional_override`, etc.
  - Conversion logic scattered: `coerce_assessment_score()` in `resolution.py:24-37`, `_coerce_subjective_score()` in `scoring.py:56-63`.
  - No single function to "get the score from an assessment" — callers must choose which converter to call.

- **Dimension Metadata**:
  - Defined in `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/base/subjective_dimensions.py:21-46` as `DISPLAY_NAMES` dict.
  - Also loaded dynamically from prompts (line 335-358).
  - Falls back to `_LEGACY_DISPLAY_NAMES` (line 47), `_LEGACY_WEIGHT_BY_DIMENSION` (line 80-86).
  - `get_dimension_metadata()` (line 404-416) returns a dict with defaults, but those defaults come from three sources: loaded metadata, legacy metadata, or computed defaults.
  - Result: Same dimension key can have different metadata depending on call order and state of caches.

- **Issue Confidence**:
  - Represented as `Confidence` enum (`base/enums.py:12-15`) with string values.
  - But in `concerns.py`, code tests `f.get("confidence", "")` and compares as strings.
  - In `resolution.py:30`, coerces via `.get("score")`.
  - No consistent accessor — different modules use different patterns.

**Impact**: Conversion bugs, cache invalidation issues, silent type coercions. Hard to refactor safely.

---

### 4. **MISSING INVARIANTS / WEAK CONTRACTS**

**Problem**: Critical assumptions are encoded as comments or defensive code, not as type constraints or explicit preconditions.

**Specific Examples**:

- **Issue ID uniqueness**:
  - The `Issue` TypedDict (`state.py:42-64`) requires `id: str` and `file: str` etc., but nothing prevents two issues from having the same ID.
  - `upsert_issues()` in `merge_issues.py` creates IDs but there's no invariant enforcer.
  - `validate_state_invariants()` (line 308) checks `issue.get("id") != issue_id` (line 319) but only after load—IDs can diverge during runtime mutations.

- **Detector registry consistency**:
  - `base/registry.py` defines `DETECTORS` dict and `_DISPLAY_ORDER` list.
  - `register_detector()` (line 356) appends to `_DISPLAY_ORDER` but doesn't validate that all DETECTORS are in DISPLAY_ORDER or vice versa.
  - `dimension_action_type()` (line 400) iterates all DETECTORS but some detectors have `None` dimension (`concerns`, `review` at line 91-93 in `_scoring/policy/core.py`). No invariant enforces "non-None dimension for scoring detectors".

- **Plan/State coupling**:
  - `reconcile_plan_after_scan()` (`_plan/reconcile.py:?) expects both state and plan to contain certain fields, but if state is empty or plan is corrupted, it silently produces partial results.
  - Line 42 in `reconcile.py`: `state.get("issues", {})` — silently assumes issues is a dict if missing, but what if it's a list? No exception raised.

**Impact**: Silent data corruption, invariant violations, cascading failures far from root cause.

---

### 5. **REDUNDANT VALIDATION / DEFENSIVE CODING**

**Problem**: Same validations repeated across modules. Runtime type-checks even though TypedDicts exist.

**Specific Examples**:

- **Repeated dict-type checks**:
  - Line 261-262 in `schema.py` (`ensure_state_defaults`): `if not isinstance(state.get("subjective_integrity"), dict):`
  - Line 301: `if integrity is not None and not isinstance(integrity, dict):`
  - Line 310-312 in `validate_state_invariants`: `if not isinstance(state.get("issues"), dict):`
  - Scattered across `_state/` modules without a shared validation layer.

- **Repeated issue-status validation**:
  - Line 321-324 in `schema.py`: `if issue.get("status") not in _ALLOWED_ISSUE_STATUSES:`
  - Same check in `filtering.py:66-67` (`issue["status"] != "open"`) but without the "allowed" validation.
  - `_mark_auto_resolved()` in `merge_issues.py:50-62` directly sets `issue["status"] = "auto_resolved"` without calling any validator.

- **Repeated issue-detail type checks**:
  - Line 143 in `concerns.py`: `detail = detail_raw if isinstance(detail_raw, dict) else {}`
  - Line 170: Same pattern.
  - Line 491-492: Same pattern.
  - No shared `_get_detail_dict()` helper.

**Impact**: Bug-prone copy-paste. Changes to validation logic must be propagated manually. Test coverage fragmented.

---

### 6. **LEAKY ABSTRACTIONS / LAYER VIOLATIONS**

**Problem**: Higher-level modules directly manipulate lower-level data structures, exposing implementation details.

**Specific Examples**:

- **Plan module knows state schema details**:
  - `_plan/reconcile.py:52` calls `state.get("issues", {})` directly.
  - `_plan/stale_dimensions.py:20` directly accesses `state.get("dimension_scores", {})`.
  - Should go through a state API layer, not direct dict access.

- **Narrative module directly manipulates state**:
  - `intelligence/narrative/core.py:80-85` directly accesses `state.get("scan_history", [])`, `state.get("dimension_scores", {})`, `state.get("stats", {})`.
  - No `state_api` layer abstracting these accesses.

- **Review module knows concern schema**:
  - `app/commands/review/importing/cmd.py` (not shown) presumably imports concern types, but concerns are defined in `engine/concerns.py` which is below the review layer.

- **Issue detail dict is heterogeneous**:
  - Different detectors put different keys in `issue["detail"]`:
    - `structural` puts `{"loc": int, "complexity_signals": list[str]}` (line 147, `concerns.py`)
    - `smells` puts `{"smell_id": str, "function": str}` (line 154, `concerns.py`)
  - No schema per detector. Callers must know detector-specific shapes.

**Impact**: Cascading changes when state schema evolves. Difficult to refactor. Hard to isolate layers for testing.

---

### 7. **INCONSISTENT ERROR HANDLING**

**Problem**: Some errors are raised, some are silently caught, some are logged but not propagated. No unified error contract.

**Specific Examples**:

- **Silent load failures**:
  - `persistence.py:55-108` loads state JSON. If JSON is corrupted, it falls back to backup (line 61), then returns `empty_state()` (line 90).
  - No exception raised, no return code to indicate failure. Caller gets an empty state and can't tell if the load succeeded.

- **Selective exception catching**:
  - `_lang/base.py` (or wherever language plugins are loaded) probably catches `ImportError` when a language is missing.
  - But `cli.py:88` catches `(OSError, KeyError, ValueError, TypeError, AttributeError)` to silence failures in `_resolve_default_path()`.
  - Different modules catch different exception sets with no shared pattern.

- **Runtime assertions disguised as checks**:
  - `concerns.py:104` filters issues by `f.get("detector", "") in JUDGMENT_DETECTORS` without asserting all issues have a detector. If a detector is missing, it silently gets a blank string and skips the issue.

**Impact**: Bugs hidden by silent failures. Unhandled exceptions in one layer crash higher layers. Hard to debug.

---

### 8. **TYPE COERCIONS WITHOUT EXPLICIT NARROWING**

**Problem**: Functions accept broad types (dict, object, int | float) and coerce internally with no static guarantee of success.

**Specific Examples**:

- **Coerce to float**:
  - Line 31 in `resolution.py`: `round(max(0.0, min(100.0, float(value))))` — `value` is `object`, could be anything.
  - No explicit type guard before `float()` call. If it fails, caller sees a TypeError.
  - Repeated in `scoring.py:60` with same signature.

- **Coerce to int**:
  - Line 214 in `schema.py`: `_as_non_negative_int(value: Any, default: int = 0) -> int:`
  - Tries `int(value)`, catches `TypeError` and `ValueError`, returns `default`.
  - But `Any` masks what types are actually valid. Caller doesn't know if they should pass int/float/string.

- **Coerce to dict**:
  - Line 43 in `persistence.py`: `cast(StateModel, data)` — casts `object` to `StateModel`.
  - No type check between the cast and use. If the cast is wrong, downstream code fails silently.

**Impact**: Unsafe type conversions. Runtime errors instead of static errors. No way to reason about what types are valid.

---

### 9. **CONTEXT OBJECTS WITH OPAQUE, GROWING SIGNATURES**

**Problem**: Context/payload objects grow over time without clear ownership of which field is required vs optional. Callers don't know what they must populate.

**Specific Examples**:

- **`NarrativeContext`** (`intelligence/narrative/core.py:56-64`):
  - All fields are `Optional` with `None` defaults.
  - `compute_narrative()` (line 67) takes this, unpacks into locals, then passes pieces around.
  - If caller forgets to pass `plan`, does it silently not compute actions? Or fail? Nowhere documented.

- **`ActionContext`** (`intelligence/narrative/action_models.py`, not shown but referenced in `core.py:95-100`):
  - Passed to `compute_actions()` but signature not inspected here.
  - Presumably has many optional fields. Callers must guess what's needed.

- **Review `ReviewIssuePayload`** (`intelligence/review/importing/contracts.py:20-36`):
  - TypedDict with `total=False` (all optional).
  - But `validate_review_issue_payload()` (line 57) enforces `REVIEW_ISSUE_REQUIRED_FIELDS` at runtime.
  - Why aren't these `Required` in the TypedDict? Answer: to allow dismissed issues to skip fields (line 68-94).
  - But that means the type doesn't match the contract. Callers creating ReviewIssuePayload can't use type checking.

**Impact**: Runtime validation instead of static. Callers don't know what they must provide. Silent failures if required fields are omitted.

---

### 10. **DETECTOR METADATA SPLIT ACROSS MODULES**

**Problem**: A single detector's configuration is scattered across multiple files with no single source of truth.

**Specific Examples**:

- **`unused` detector**:
  - Defined in `base/registry.py:63-69` with metadata.
  - Policy in `_scoring/policy/core.py:45` sets `dimension="Code quality"`, `tier=3`.
  - Subjective impacts in `_state/merge.py:37-52` (marks dims stale when changed).
  - Display logic in `app/output/scorecard_parts/...` (not inspected in detail).
  - Fixers in `languages/typescript/_get_ts_fixers()` (not shown, but mentioned in memory).
  - If you want to understand "unused" fully, you must visit 5+ files.

- **`review` detector**:
  - Metadata in `base/registry.py:323-328`.
  - Policy in `_scoring/policy/core.py:93` sets `dimension=None`.
  - But merge.py doesn't mention it (so no subjective marking on review changes? or is that intentional?).
  - Used in `_state/merge_issues.py:30` as import-only.

**Impact**: Bugs when one file is updated but others aren't. Hard to understand detector behavior. Refactoring a detector is error-prone.

---

### 11. **DIMENSION SCORES TREATED AS BOTH STATE AND COMPUTED**

**Problem**: `state["dimension_scores"]` is persisted but sometimes treated as computed/read-only, sometimes as mutable.

**Specific Examples**:

- **Stored in state JSON** but usage is inconsistent:
  - `_state/merge.py` doesn't directly populate `dimension_scores` — that's done elsewhere.
  - `_state/scoring.py:` doesn't see `dimension_scores` at all (only stats).
  - But `intelligence/narrative/core.py:82` reads `state.get("dimension_scores", {})`.
  - And `_plan/stale_dimensions.py` checks `dimension_scores` existence.

- **Not in TypedDict**:
  - `StateModel` in `schema.py` doesn't list `dimension_scores` as a field (though line 154 mentions `score_confidence`).
  - But code assumes it exists and is a dict.
  - Actual shape: `dict[str, dict[str, Any]]` (dimension key → detector counts).

**Impact**: Silent bugs if dimension_scores is missing or malformed. No single schema. Unclear when it's populated.

---

### 12. **PLAN AND STATE USE INCONSISTENT DEFAULTS FOR MISSING CONTAINERS**

**Problem**: When accessing nested dicts, some code uses `.get(key, {})`, some uses `.get(key)` then type-checks, some uses `.setdefault()`. No uniform pattern.

**Specific Examples**:

- **state.get("issues", {})** appears ~10+ times.
- **plan.get("queue_order", [])** appears multiple times.
- **state.setdefault("scan_history", [])** in `schema.py:255`.
- But **state.get("subjective_assessments")** is often checked with `isinstance()` before use (e.g., `resolution.py:53-54`).

- Why inconsistent? Different intent:
  - `.get(key, {})` says "I assume this is always a dict, return empty if missing".
  - `.get(key)` + isinstance check says "I want to handle the case where this might not be a dict".

**Impact**: Silent type mismatches. Code can't assume shape of returned value without checking. Scattered validation.

---

### 13. **CIRCULAR CONFIGURATION AND RUNTIME REGISTRATION**

**Problem**: Detector registry can be extended at runtime, but some code caches detectors, creating potential staleness.

**Specific Examples**:

- **`base/registry.py:348-365`** (`register_detector`, `reset_registered_detectors`):
  - Global `DETECTORS` dict is mutated at runtime.
  - `_DISPLAY_ORDER` list is mutated at runtime.
  - On registration, callbacks are fired (line 365-366).

- **`cli.py:23-39`** (`_get_detector_names`):
  - Caches detector names using `_DETECTOR_NAMES_CACHE` dict.
  - Invalidated on registration via `on_detector_registered(_invalidate_detector_names_cache)`.
  - But what about other cached views? `intelligence/narrative/_constants.py:16` caches `DETECTOR_TOOLS` and refreshes on registration (line 26).

- **Problem**: If someone calls `register_detector()` but forgets to call `_refresh_detector_tools()`, DETECTOR_TOOLS becomes stale.
  - No static enforcement. Easy to miss.

**Impact**: Subtle concurrency/initialization bugs. Runtime inconsistency. Hard to test plugins.

---

### 14. **PLAN OPERATIONS HAVE MIXED RETURN PATTERNS**

**Problem**: Some operations return a count/summary, some return the modified plan, some return None.

**Specific Examples**:

- **`_plan/reconcile.py:28-35`** (`ReconcileResult`):
  - `reconcile_plan_after_scan()` returns a custom dataclass with change counts.
  - Good: clear what changed.

- **`_plan/operations_skip.py`** (not inspected in detail, but likely similar):
  - Probably returns nothing or returns the modified plan.

- **`_plan/commit_tracking.py`** (not inspected in detail):
  - Probably returns issue lists or commit records.

- **Problem**: Callers must know each operation's return type. No uniform contract.
  - "Does this operation return the modified plan, or do I need to access the plan separately?"
  - "Does this operation return a summary of changes, or do I need to diff plan state myself?"

**Impact**: Caller confusion. Inconsistent usage patterns. Hard to compose operations.

---

### 15. **SURVEY AND RECONCILIATION SPREAD ACROSS MODULES**

**Problem**: The logic to "survey what changed during scan" and "reconcile plan with state" is split across `_state/merge.py`, `_state/merge_issues.py`, and `_plan/reconcile.py` with no clear separation of concerns.

**Specific Examples**:

- **`_state/merge.py:54-94`** contains:
  - `_mark_stale_on_mechanical_change()` — marks subjective assessments stale when detector issues change.
  - But this is state concerns, not merge concerns.

- **`_state/merge_issues.py:9-46`** (`find_suspect_detectors`):
  - Figures out which detectors "probably didn't run" based on issue counts.
  - But this is really planning logic, not merging logic.

- **`_plan/reconcile.py:37-115`** (`_supersede_id`):
  - Handles the case where an issue disappeared from plan but not state.
  - But issue lifecycle is state concerns, not plan concerns.

**Impact**: Hard to understand the full flow. State and plan changes are entangled. Testing requires mocking multiple layers.

---

### 16. **CASTING WITHOUT VALIDATION**

**Problem**: Uses `cast()` to narrow types without actual verification.

**Specific Examples**:

- **`persistence.py:44`**: `normalized = cast(StateModel, data)`
  - After `ensure_state_defaults()` on line 43, the code trusts that `data` is now a valid `StateModel`.
  - But if `ensure_state_defaults()` is buggy or incomplete, the cast is wrong.
  - No static guarantee. No runtime assertion.

- **`resolution.py:87-93`** (`match_issues`):
  - Returns list of dicts from `state["issues"].items()` as a list of `dict`.
  - No cast, but the type is loose.

**Impact**: Type narrowing without verification. Downstream code can fail if the cast assumption is wrong.

---

### 17. **SCAN HISTORY TREATED AS IMMUTABLE BUT MUTATED DURING LOAD**

**Problem**: `scan_history` is loaded as a list, entries are type-checked and potentially modified during load, but the list itself is never re-validated.

**Specific Examples**:

- **`schema.py:297-302`** (`ensure_state_defaults`):
  - Iterates `state["scan_history"]`, checks each entry, fixes `subjective_integrity` field.
  - But doesn't return the count of modifications or validate the result.

- **`merge_history.py:99-121`** (likely appending to scan_history):
  - Builds a new history entry, appends to `state["scan_history"]`.
  - But the entry might not conform to `ScanHistoryEntry` TypedDict.

**Impact**: scan_history can contain entries with missing/wrong fields. Silent data corruption.

---

### 18. **CONCERN DISMISSALS COUPLE TO ASSUMPTION ABOUT source_issue_ids EXISTENCE**

**Problem**: `_is_dismissed()` in `concerns.py:77-85` assumes dismissals always have `source_issue_ids`, but `cleanup_stale_dismissals()` at line 572 explicitly checks for its existence.

**Specific Examples**:

- Line 572: `if entry.get("source_issue_ids")` — acknowledges that legacy dismissals might not have this field.
- But line 84: `prev_sources = set(entry.get("source_issue_ids", []))` — silently defaults to empty if missing.
- Result: Legacy dismissals without `source_issue_ids` are never matched (always return `False`), so they're not cleaned up until `cleanup_stale_dismissals()` is called.
- Is this intentional? Nowhere documented.

**Impact**: Silent behavior change between concern generators and cleanup. Data migration issues.

---

### 19. **MISSING TYPE NARROWING AFTER RUNTIME CHECKS**

**Problem**: After checking `isinstance(..., dict)`, code still accesses the value as if it could be non-dict in some paths.

**Specific Examples**:

- **`concerns.py:142-143`** (in `_extract_signals`):
  ```python
  detail_raw = f.get("detail", {})
  detail = detail_raw if isinstance(detail_raw, dict) else {}
  ```
  - Good: narrows type explicitly.

- **But `concerns.py:170`** does the same pattern again instead of defining a helper.

- **`resolution.py:53-55`**:
  ```python
  assessments = state.get("subjective_assessments")
  if not isinstance(assessments, dict) or not assessments:
      return
  ```
  - After the check, `assessments` is guaranteed to be a non-empty dict.
  - But if someone copies this code later and forgets the non-empty check, silent bugs.

**Impact**: Repeated type narrowing code. Easy to miss edge cases. Not DRY.

---

### 20. **StateModel AND PlanModel LACK COMPLETE SCHEMA DOCUMENTATION**

**Problem**: TypedDict definitions don't capture all fields that code uses, and optional field semantics are unclear.

**Specific Examples**:

- **`StateModel`** in `schema.py:142-158`:
  - Lists `version`, `created`, `last_scan`, `scan_count`, `overall_score`, `objective_score`, `strict_score`, `verified_strict_score`, `stats`, `issues`, `scan_coverage`, `score_confidence`, `scan_history`, `subjective_integrity`, `subjective_assessments`, `concern_dismissals`.
  - But code also accesses `state["scan_path"]` (line 20 in `merge_history.py`), `state["tool_hash"]` (line 19), `state["scan_completeness"]` (line 22), `state["potentials"]` (line 36), `state["codebase_metrics"]` (line 45).
  - These fields are not in the TypedDict. So type checkers won't catch access errors.

- **`PlanModel`** in `_plan/schema.py:100-?`:
  - Read past where the file snippet ends, but likely has the same issue.

**Impact**: Type checkers can't validate all accesses. Silent field additions/removals. No single source of truth for state shape.

---

### SUMMARY TABLE

| Issue Category | Severity | Cognitive Load | Examples |
|---|---|---|---|
| Implicit mutations returning None | High | Very High | ensure_state_defaults, _record_scan_metadata |
| Implicit optional parameters | High | High | path_scoped_issues, auto_resolve_disappeared |
| Multiple representations of same concept | High | Very High | subjective dimension scores, detector confidence |
| Missing invariants | High | High | issue ID uniqueness, detector registry consistency |
| Redundant validation | Medium | Medium | repeated isinstance checks, repeated status validation |
| Leaky abstractions | Medium | High | plan accessing state["issues"] directly |
| Inconsistent error handling | Medium | High | silent load failures, selective exception catching |
| Type coercions without narrowing | Medium | Medium | float(object), cast without validation |
| Context objects with opaque fields | Medium | High | NarrativeContext, ReviewIssuePayload |
| Detector metadata split across modules | High | Very High | unused detector defined in 5+ files |
| dimension_scores treated as both state and computed | Medium | High | persisted but not in TypedDict, unclear population |
| Inconsistent container defaults | Low | Medium | .get(key, {}) vs .get(key) + isinstance |
| Circular configuration and caching | Medium | High | detector registration + caching + invalidation |
| Plan operations with mixed returns | Low | Medium | some return counts, some return plan |
| Survey and reconciliation entangled | Medium | Very High | state/merge logic spread across 3 modules |
| Casting without validation | Medium | Medium | cast(StateModel, data) |
| scan_history mutated during load | Medium | Medium | ensure_state_defaults modifies entries |
| Concern dismissals and source_issue_ids | Medium | Medium | legacy entries don't have the field |
| Missing type narrowing after checks | Low | Low | repeated isinstance + narrowing logic |
| Schema documentation incomplete | Medium | High | StateModel doesn't list all fields actually used |

---

This catalog exposes a codebase that works but requires significant cognitive load to understand safely. The core tension is between **dynamic dict-based schemas** (flexible but untyped) and **TypedDict-based contracts** (static but loose), with extensive runtime defensive code to bridge the gap.
